People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doc, it's nice to meet you too. I guess what brings me in today is... well, it's this constant moving around I've been doing. You see, I retired not too long ago, and you'd think I'd be relaxing, sitting on the porch with a cup of coffee, right? But no, since I stopped working, I just can’t seem to slow down. It's like my engine is still running on high. I wake up at the crack of dawn, same as always, and I'm just up and moving from one little job around the house to the next, all day long. 

I'll start fixing a hinge on the cabinet, and before I'm even done with that, I'm out in the yard pulling weeds, and then I'm in the garage organizing tools I haven't used in ten years. My wife says it's exhausting just watching me. It's almost like I still have a whole crew waiting on me to give them orders and keep things moving, you know? Back when I was working, that energy was good, it got things done. But now? Now there's no crew, no big project, just me and a hundred half-finished chores. I just can't sit still for five minutes without feeling like I need to be doing *something*.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not exactly less sleep. I mean, I still need my solid seven or eight hours, same as I always have. It’s not like I’m surviving on three hours and bouncing off the walls, feeling like Superman or anything. If I don't get a good night's rest, oh boy, I feel it the next day. My joints ache more, my head feels a bit foggy, and I’m just dragging my feet. 

It’s more like... once my eyes open, whether it's 5:30 or 6:00 AM, that’s it. The switch is flipped. I can't just lay there in bed and enjoy the morning like my wife does. She can lay there for an hour, just dozing or listening to the birds. Me? The second I'm awake, my brain starts making a list. 'Gotta check the gutters, gotta oil that squeaky door, gotta see if the hardware store has that specific grade of sandpaper.' So I have to get up. But I definitely still need the sleep itself to keep going. I just don't have an 'off' switch once I'm actually awake, if that makes sense. It's not that I need *less* rest, I just can't seem to rest when I'm awake.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really worrying. It's not like my mind is spinning about, you know, paying the bills or whether the roof is going to leak or if the grandkids are doing okay in school. My wife, now, she’s a worrier. She’ll sit in her armchair with her knitting and fret about everything under the sun. But for me, it’s not anxiety or fear or anything like that. 

It’s more... well, it’s just this physical itch, I guess you could call it. Like, if I sit down in the recliner to watch the evening news—which I try to do, I really do, because my wife likes us to sit together—I might make it through the first commercial break. But then my leg starts bouncing. Just bouncing up and down, tapping the floor. And then I start looking around the room. I’ll notice that the picture frame on the mantel is just a hair crooked, or I’ll remember that I left my good tape measure out on the workbench instead of putting it back in the drawer. 

And it’s not that I’m worried the tape measure is going to rust or get stolen, it’s just that the thought of it sitting there out of place just nags at me. It just nags and nags until I have to get up and fix it. So I’ll tell my wife, "Just going to the kitchen for a glass of water," but really I’m walking all the way out to the garage to put the tape measure away. And while I’m there, I might sweep up a little sawdust. And then thirty minutes later, she’s hollering for me because the news is over and Jeopardy is starting. So no, it’s not worry, doc. It’s just this... this absolute inability to just sit and exist without doing something with my hands or my feet.

Back when I was working, that energy was good, it got things done. But now? Now there's no crew, no big project, just me and a hundred half-finished chores. I just can't sit still for five minutes without feeling like I need to be doing *something*.\n\nDoctor: Do you need less sleep than usual and still feel full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly less sleep. I mean, I still need my solid seven or eight hours, same as I always have. It’s not like I’m surviving on three hours and bouncing off the walls, feeling like Superman or anything. If I don't get a good night's rest, oh boy, I feel it the next day. My joints ache more, my head feels a bit foggy, and I’m just dragging my feet. \n\nIt’s more like... once my eyes open, whether it's 5:30 or 6:00 AM, that’s it. The switch is flipped. I can't just lay there in bed and enjoy the morning like my wife does. She can lay there for an hour, just dozing or listening to the birds. Me? The second I'm awake, my brain starts making a list. 'Gotta check the gutters, gotta oil that squeaky door, gotta see if the hardware store has that specific grade of sandpaper.' So I have to get up. But I definitely still need the sleep itself to keep going. I just don't have an 'off' switch once I'm actually awake, if that makes sense. It's not that I need *less* rest, I just can't seem to rest when I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: When you try to sit still and relax, do you start worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really worrying. It's not like my mind is spinning about, you know, paying the bills or whether the roof is going to leak or if the grandkids are doing okay in school. My wife, now, she’s a worrier. She’ll sit in her armchair with her knitting and fret about everything under the sun. But for me, it’s not anxiety or fear or anything like that. \n\nIt’s more... well, it’s just this physical itch, I guess you could call it. Like, if I sit down in the recliner to watch the evening news—which I try to do, I really do, because my wife likes us to sit together—I might make it through the first commercial break. But then my leg starts bouncing. Just bouncing up and down, tapping the floor. And then I start looking around the room. I’ll notice that the picture frame on the mantel is just a hair crooked, or I’ll remember that I left my good tape measure out on the workbench instead of putting it back in the drawer. \n\nAnd it’s not that I’m worried the tape measure is going to rust or get stolen, it’s just that the thought of it sitting there out of place just nags at me. It just nags and nags until I have to get up and fix it. So I’ll tell my wife, \"Just going to the kitchen for a glass of water,\" but really I’m walking all the way out to the garage to put the tape measure away. And while I’m there, I might sweep up a little sawdust. And then thirty minutes later, she’s hollering for me because the news is over and Jeopardy is starting. So no, it’s not worry, doc. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. Yes, I absolutely was. I mean, if you ask my older sister, she'll tell you I was a terror, but that's just sisters for you. But seriously, looking back, even way back in grade school—I went to this little brick schoolhouse, Lincoln Elementary, they tore it down back in the eighties to build a strip mall, which is a shame, really, it had these beautiful big windows—but anyway, my teachers there, they always had the same thing to say to my parents. 

I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She was this strict older lady, always wore these heavy wool cardigans even in May. She told my mother, she said, "He's a bright boy, he really is, but he's just... scattered." That was her word, scattered. And she wasn't wrong. I was always, always losing my pencils. My dad must have bought me a hundred of those yellow Ticonderoga pencils a year. I’d set one down to look at a bug on the windowsill, and poof, it was gone. And I couldn't just sit quietly and raise my hand. If I knew the answer, or even if I just thought I knew it, I'd just blurt it right out. "George Washington!" "The capital of Ohio is Columbus!" I just couldn't hold it in. 

And finishing my work? Oh, boy. It wasn't that the work was too hard, it was just... there were so many other things to look at. The kid next to me tying his shoe, the clock ticking on the wall, a dog barking outside. Mrs. Gable would have to come stand right next to my desk, tap her finger on my paper, and say, "You need to finish this math sheet before you can go out for recess." And of course, I wanted to go to recess more than anything in the world, because that meant running around and playing kickball, but I still needed that constant reminding just to get through ten multiplication problems. So, yeah, I guess that engine has been running hot for a very, very long time. It’s just who I am, really. Always has been.

So, yeah, I guess that engine has been running hot for a very, very long time. It’s just who I am, really. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose it has. I mean, if you really look at the whole picture of it. It’s not just one thing, you know? It’s kind of bled into everything over the years. 

Take things at home, for instance. My wife, Mary—she’s an absolute saint, really she is. We’ve been married forty-two years this coming October, and I don't know where I'd be without her. She’s completely taken over keeping the calendar. Used to be we’d both try to keep track, but I’d double-book us or just flat-out forget. I’d say I was going to fix the back gate on Saturday, but then I’d get sidetracked organizing the tool shed, and by Sunday the gate was still swinging loose. So now, she just handles it all. She checks behind me, too. If I say I’ve paid the electric bill, she’ll just quietly log in and make sure I actually hit 'submit' and didn't just get up halfway through to go check the mail and forget all about it. It’s a bit embarrassing, really, a man my age needing his wife to double-check his chores, but she says she doesn't mind. Though I know it tires her out sometimes.

And then outside the house... well, since I retired from the plant, I try to stay busy. I joined the community center board, and I’m part of this local garden group. We maintain the flowerbeds down by the library and the town square. It’s good work, gets you out in the sunshine. But, oh boy, I’ve had to apologize to both those groups more times than I care to count. Just... letting people down, you know? I’ll volunteer to pick up the mulch for the garden group, and then the day comes, and I’ve completely forgotten because I was busy power-washing the driveway, which didn't even really need power-washing, but I just had to do it right then. Or at the community center meetings, I’ll get so restless sitting in those hard plastic folding chairs that I’ll start pacing in the back of the room, and I know it distracts the speaker. Or I’ll interrupt someone because an idea popped into my head and I just couldn't wait my turn. 

So, yeah, it’s not just a home thing or just a school thing back in the day. It’s... it’s kind of everywhere. Always has been. I just try to keep moving and hope I don't step on too many toes along the way, you know?
=====================================

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n5\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F41.1, F43.2, F60.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, it's nice to meet you too. I guess what brings me in today is... well, it's this constant moving around I've been doing. You see, I retired not too long ago, and you'd think I'd be relaxing, sitting on the porch with a cup of coffee, right? But no, since I stopped working, I just can’t seem to slow down. It's like my engine is still running on high. I wake up at the crack of dawn, same as always, and I'm just up and moving from one little job around the house to the next, all day long. \n\nI'll start fixing a hinge on the cabinet, and before I'm even done with that, I'm out in the yard pulling weeds, and then I'm in the garage organizing tools I haven't used in ten years. My wife says it's exhausting just watching me. It's almost like I still have a whole crew waiting on me to give them orders and keep things moving, you know? Back when I was working, that energy was good, it got things done. But now? Now there's no crew, no big project, just me and a hundred half-finished chores. I just can't sit still for five minutes without feeling like I need to be doing *something*.\n\nDoctor: Do you need less sleep than usual and still feel full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly less sleep. I mean, I still need my solid seven or eight hours, same as I always have. It’s not like I’m surviving on three hours and bouncing off the walls, feeling like Superman or anything. If I don't get a good night's rest, oh boy, I feel it the next day. My joints ache more, my head feels a bit foggy, and I’m just dragging my feet. \n\nIt’s more like... once my eyes open, whether it's 5:30 or 6:00 AM, that’s it. The switch is flipped. I can't just lay there in bed and enjoy the morning like my wife does. She can lay there for an hour, just dozing or listening to the birds. Me? The second I'm awake, my brain starts making a list. 'Gotta check the gutters, gotta oil that squeaky door, gotta see if the hardware store has that specific grade of sandpaper.' So I have to get up. But I definitely still need the sleep itself to keep going. I just don't have an 'off' switch once I'm actually awake, if that makes sense. It's not that I need *less* rest, I just can't seem to rest when I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: When you try to sit still and relax, do you start worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really worrying. It's not like my mind is spinning about, you know, paying the bills or whether the roof is going to leak or if the grandkids are doing okay in school. My wife, now, she’s a worrier. She’ll sit in her armchair with her knitting and fret about everything under the sun. But for me, it’s not anxiety or fear or anything like that. \n\nIt’s more... well, it’s just this physical itch, I guess you could call it. Like, if I sit down in the recliner to watch the evening news—which I try to do, I really do, because my wife likes us to sit together—I might make it through the first commercial break. But then my leg starts bouncing. Just bouncing up and down, tapping the floor. And then I start looking around the room. I’ll notice that the picture frame on the mantel is just a hair crooked, or I’ll remember that I left my good tape measure out on the workbench instead of putting it back in the drawer. \n\nAnd it’s not that I’m worried the tape measure is going to rust or get stolen, it’s just that the thought of it sitting there out of place just nags at me. It just nags and nags until I have to get up and fix it. So I’ll tell my wife, \"Just going to the kitchen for a glass of water,\" but really I’m walking all the way out to the garage to put the tape measure away. And while I’m there, I might sweep up a little sawdust. And then thirty minutes later, she’s hollering for me because the news is over and Jeopardy is starting. So no, it’s not worry, doc. It’s just this... this absolute inability to just sit and exist without doing something with my hands or my feet.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to when you were a child, were you already the kind of person who had trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. Yes, I absolutely was. I mean, if you ask my older sister, she'll tell you I was a terror, but that's just sisters for you. But seriously, looking back, even way back in grade school—I went to this little brick schoolhouse, Lincoln Elementary, they tore it down back in the eighties to build a strip mall, which is a shame, really, it had these beautiful big windows—but anyway, my teachers there, they always had the same thing to say to my parents. \n\nI remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She was this strict older lady, always wore these heavy wool cardigans even in May. She told my mother, she said, \"He's a bright boy, he really is, but he's just... scattered.\" That was her word, scattered. And she wasn't wrong. I was always, always losing my pencils. My dad must have bought me a hundred of those yellow Ticonderoga pencils a year. I’d set one down to look at a bug on the windowsill, and poof, it was gone. And I couldn't just sit quietly and raise my hand. If I knew the answer, or even if I just thought I knew it, I'd just blurt it right out. \"George Washington!\" \"The capital of Ohio is Columbus!\" I just couldn't hold it in. \n\nAnd finishing my work? Oh, boy. It wasn't that the work was too hard, it was just... there were so many other things to look at. The kid next to me tying his shoe, the clock ticking on the wall, a dog barking outside. Mrs. Gable would have to come stand right next to my desk, tap her finger on my paper, and say, \"You need to finish this math sheet before you can go out for recess.\" And of course, I wanted to go to recess more than anything in the world, because that meant running around and playing kickball, but I still needed that constant reminding just to get through ten multiplication problems. So, yeah, I guess that engine has been running hot for a very, very long time. It’s just who I am, really. Always has been.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with sitting still and staying focused caused trouble for you in more than one part of your life, like at school, at work, or at home?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has. I mean, if you really look at the whole picture of it. It’s not just one thing, you know? It’s kind of bled into everything over the years. \n\nTake things at home, for instance. My wife, Mary—she’s an absolute saint, really she is. We’ve been married forty-two years this coming October, and I don't know where I'd be without her. She’s completely taken over keeping the calendar. Used to be we’d both try to keep track, but I’d double-book us or just flat-out forget. I’d say I was going to fix the back gate on Saturday, but then I’d get sidetracked organizing the tool shed, and by Sunday the gate was still swinging loose. So now, she just handles it all. She checks behind me, too. If I say I’ve paid the electric bill, she’ll just quietly log in and make sure I actually hit 'submit' and didn't just get up halfway through to go check the mail and forget all about it. It’s a bit embarrassing, really, a man my age needing his wife to double-check his chores, but she says she doesn't mind. Though I know it tires her out sometimes.\n\nAnd then outside the house... well, since I retired from the plant, I try to stay busy. I joined the community center board, and I’m part of this local garden group. We maintain the flowerbeds down by the library and the town square. It’s good work, gets you out in the sunshine. But, oh boy, I’ve had to apologize to both those groups more times than I care to count. Just... letting people down, you know? I’ll volunteer to pick up the mulch for the garden group, and then the day comes, and I’ve completely forgotten because I was busy power-washing the driveway, which didn't even really need power-washing, but I just had to do it right then. Or at the community center meetings, I’ll get so restless sitting in those hard plastic folding chairs that I’ll start pacing in the back of the room, and I know it distracts the speaker. Or I’ll interrupt someone because an idea popped into my head and I just couldn't wait my turn. \n\nSo, yeah, it’s not just a home thing or just a school thing back in the day. It’s... it’s kind of everywhere. Always has been. I just try to keep moving and hope I don't step on too many toes along the way, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’d say I was going to fix the back gate on Saturday, but then I’d get sidetracked organizing the tool shed, and by Sunday the gate was still swinging loose. So now, she just handles it all. She checks behind me, too. If I say I’ve paid the electric bill, she’ll just quietly log in and make sure I actually hit 'submit' and didn't just get up halfway through to go check the mail and forget all about it. It’s a bit embarrassing, really, a man my age needing his wife to double-check his chores, but she says she doesn't mind. Though I know it tires her out sometimes.\n\nAnd then outside the house... well, since I retired from the plant, I try to stay busy. I joined the community center board, and I’m part of this local garden group. We maintain the flowerbeds down by the library and the town square. It’s good work, gets you out in the sunshine. But, oh boy, I’ve had to apologize to both those groups more times than I care to count. Just... letting people down, you know? I’ll volunteer to pick up the mulch for the garden group, and then the day comes, and I’ve completely forgotten because I was busy power-washing the driveway, which didn't even really need power-washing, but I just had to do it right then. Or at the community center meetings, I’ll get so restless sitting in those hard plastic folding chairs that I’ll start pacing in the back of the room, and I know it distracts the speaker. Or I’ll interrupt someone because an idea popped into my head and I just couldn't wait my turn. \n\nSo, yeah, it’s not just a home thing or just a school thing back in the day. It’s... it’s kind of everywhere. Always has been. I just try to keep moving and hope I don't step on too many toes along the way, you know?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel a strong need for things to be done in a very specific, exact way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 6\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

Yes, I absolutely was. I mean, if you ask my older sister, she'll tell you I was a terror, but that's just sisters for you. But seriously, looking back, even way back in grade school—I went to this little brick schoolhouse, Lincoln Elementary, they tore it down back in the eighties to build a strip mall, which is a shame, really, it had these beautiful big windows—but anyway, my teachers there, they always had the same thing to say to my parents. \n\nI remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She was this strict older lady, always wore these heavy wool cardigans even in May. She told my mother, she said, \"He's a bright boy, he really is, but he's just... scattered.\" That was her word, scattered. And she wasn't wrong. I was always, always losing my pencils. My dad must have bought me a hundred of those yellow Ticonderoga pencils a year. I’d set one down to look at a bug on the windowsill, and poof, it was gone. And I couldn't just sit quietly and raise my hand. If I knew the answer, or even if I just thought I knew it, I'd just blurt it right out. \"George Washington!\" \"The capital of Ohio is Columbus!\" I just couldn't hold it in. \n\nAnd finishing my work? Oh, boy. It wasn't that the work was too hard, it was just... there were so many other things to look at. The kid next to me tying his shoe, the clock ticking on the wall, a dog barking outside. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, I wouldn't say that at all. If anything, it is probably the exact opposite with me. I really don't care how something gets done, just so long as I am doing it, you know? Take my neighbor down the street, Bob. Now Bob, he used to be a draftsman or something before he retired, and when he mows his lawn, I swear to you he must use a ruler. He goes back and forth in these perfect, straight lines, and if he misses a single blade of grass, he stops the whole mower, walks back, and plucks it by hand. It is exhausting just watching the man from my front porch. 

But me, oh heavens no. I just kind of go at things however the spirit moves me. Like last week, I decided to build one of those raised planter boxes for Mary's tomatoes. I bought the kit from the hardware store, the one with the precut cedar boards and all the hardware in a little plastic bag. Well, the instructions said to lay it all out and use a level and this special drill bit they provided, but I just tossed the paper aside and started putting it together with whatever screws I had sitting in my old Folgers coffee can on the workbench. It went a lot faster my way, let me tell you. Sure, the box ended up a little lopsided, and one corner is definitely sitting higher than the rest, but I figure the tomatoes are not going to care if their bed is perfectly square. 

So, no, I don't have any sort of rigid system or need for things to be exact. I just want to get my hands dirty and get the project moving. If I had to sit there and plan out every little step and make sure it was all perfectly aligned, I think I would go absolutely crazy. I just do not have the patience for that kind of exactness, doc.

It's not that I need *less* rest, I just can't seem to rest when I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: When you try to sit still and relax, do you start worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really worrying. It's not like my mind is spinning about, you know, paying the bills or whether the roof is going to leak or if the grandkids are doing okay in school. My wife, now, she’s a worrier. She’ll sit in her armchair with her knitting and fret about everything under the sun. But for me, it’s not anxiety or fear or anything like that. \n\nIt’s more... well, it’s just this physical itch, I guess you could call it. Like, if I sit down in the recliner to watch the evening news—which I try to do, I really do, because my wife likes us to sit together—I might make it through the first commercial break. But then my leg starts bouncing. Just bouncing up and down, tapping the floor. And then I start looking around the room. I’ll notice that the picture frame on the mantel is just a hair crooked, or I’ll remember that I left my good tape measure out on the workbench instead of putting it back in the drawer. \n\nAnd it’s not that I’m worried the tape measure is going to rust or get stolen, it’s just that the thought of it sitting there out of place just nags at me. It just nags and nags until I have to get up and fix it. So I’ll tell my wife, \"Just going to the kitchen for a glass of water,\" but really I’m walking all the way out to the garage to put the tape measure away. And while I’m there, I might sweep up a little sawdust. And then thirty minutes later, she’s hollering for me because the news is over and Jeopardy is starting. So no, it’s not worry, doc. It’s just this... this absolute inability to just sit and exist without doing something with my hands or my feet.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to when you were a child, were you already the kind of person who had trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. Yes, I absolutely was. I mean, if you ask my older sister, she'll tell you I was a terror, but that's just sisters for you. But seriously, looking back, even way back in grade school—I went to this little brick schoolhouse, Lincoln Elementary, they tore it down back in the eighties to build a strip mall, which is a shame, really, it had these beautiful big windows—but anyway, my teachers there, they always had the same thing to say to my parents. \n\nI remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She was this strict older lady, always wore these heavy wool cardigans even in May. She told my mother, she said, \"He's a bright boy, he really is, but he's just... scattered.\" That was her word, scattered. And she wasn't wrong. I was always, always losing my pencils. My dad must have bought me a hundred of those yellow Ticonderoga pencils a year. I’d set one down to look at a bug on the windowsill, and poof, it was gone. And I couldn't just sit quietly and raise my hand. If I knew the answer, or even if I just thought I knew it, I'd just blurt it right out. \"George Washington!\" \"The capital of Ohio is Columbus!\" I just couldn't hold it in. \n\nAnd finishing my work? Oh, boy. It wasn't that the work was too hard, it was just... there were so many other things to look at. The kid next to me tying his shoe, the clock ticking on the wall, a dog barking outside. Mrs. Gable would have to come stand right next to my desk, tap her finger on my paper, and say, \"You need to finish this math sheet before you can go out for recess.\" And of course, I wanted to go to recess more than anything in the world, because that meant running around and playing kickball, but I still needed that constant reminding just to get through ten multiplication problems. So, yeah, I guess that engine has been running hot for a very, very long time. It’s just who I am, really. Always has been.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with sitting still and staying focused caused trouble for you in more than one part of your life, like at school, at work, or at home?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has. I mean, if you really look at the whole picture of it. It’s not just one thing, you know? It’s kind of bled into everything over the years. \n\nTake things at home, for instance. My wife, Mary—she’s an absolute saint, really she is. We’ve been married forty-two years this coming October, and I don't know where I'd be without her. She’s completely taken over keeping the calendar. Used to be we’d both try to keep track, but I’d double-book us or just flat-out forget. I’d say I was going to fix the back gate on Saturday, but then I’d get sidetracked organizing the tool shed, and by Sunday the gate was still swinging loose. So now, she just handles it all. She checks behind me, too. If I say I’ve paid the electric bill, she’ll just quietly log in and make sure I actually hit 'submit' and didn't just get up halfway through to go check the mail and forget all about it. It’s a bit embarrassing, really, a man my age needing his wife to double-check his chores, but she says she doesn't mind. Though I know it tires her out sometimes.\n\nAnd then outside the house... well, since I retired from the plant, I try to stay busy. I joined the community center board, and I’m part of this local garden group. We maintain the flowerbeds down by the library and the town square. It’s good work, gets you out in the sunshine. But, oh boy, I’ve had to apologize to both those groups more times than I care to count. Just... letting people down, you know? I’ll volunteer to pick up the mulch for the garden group, and then the day comes, and I’ve completely forgotten because I was busy power-washing the driveway, which didn't even really need power-washing, but I just had to do it right then. Or at the community center meetings, I’ll get so restless sitting in those hard plastic folding chairs that I’ll start pacing in the back of the room, and I know it distracts the speaker. Or I’ll interrupt someone because an idea popped into my head and I just couldn't wait my turn. \n\nSo, yeah, it’s not just a home thing or just a school thing back in the day. It’s... it’s kind of everywhere. Always has been. I just try to keep moving and hope I don't step on too many toes along the way, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need for things to be done in a very specific, exact way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that at all. If anything, it is probably the exact opposite with me. I really don't care how something gets done, just so long as I am doing it, you know? Take my neighbor down the street, Bob. Now Bob, he used to be a draftsman or something before he retired, and when he mows his lawn, I swear to you he must use a ruler. He goes back and forth in these perfect, straight lines, and if he misses a single blade of grass, he stops the whole mower, walks back, and plucks it by hand. It is exhausting just watching the man from my front porch. \n\nBut me, oh heavens no. I just kind of go at things however the spirit moves me. Like last week, I decided to build one of those raised planter boxes for Mary's tomatoes. I bought the kit from the hardware store, the one with the precut cedar boards and all the hardware in a little plastic bag. Well, the instructions said to lay it all out and use a level and this special drill bit they provided, but I just tossed the paper aside and started putting it together with whatever screws I had sitting in my old Folgers coffee can on the workbench. It went a lot faster my way, let me tell you. Sure, the box ended up a little lopsided, and one corner is definitely sitting higher than the rest, but I figure the tomatoes are not going to care if their bed is perfectly square. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any sort of rigid system or need for things to be exact. I just want to get my hands dirty and get the project moving. If I had to sit there and plan out every little step and make sure it was all perfectly aligned, I think I would go absolutely crazy. I just do not have the patience for that kind of exactness, doc."
  }

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, doc, it's nice to meet you too. I guess what brings me in today is... well, it's this constant moving around I've been doing. You see, I retired not too long ago, and you'd think I'd be relaxing, sitting on the porch with a cup of coffee, right? But no, since I stopped working, I just can’t seem to slow down. It's like my engine is still running on high. I wake up at the crack of dawn, same as always, and I'm just up and moving from one little job around the house to the next, all day long. \n\nI'll start fixing a hinge on the cabinet, and before I'm even done with that, I'm out in the yard pulling weeds, and then I'm in the garage organizing tools I haven't used in ten years. My wife says it's exhausting just watching me. It's almost like I still have a whole crew waiting on me to give them orders and keep things moving, you know? Back when I was working, that energy was good, it got things done. But now? Now there's no crew, no big project, just me and a hundred half-finished chores. I just can't sit still for five minutes without feeling like I need to be doing *something*.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. Yes, I absolutely was. I mean, if you ask my older sister, she'll tell you I was a terror, but that's just sisters for you. But seriously, looking back, even way back in grade school—I went to this little brick schoolhouse, Lincoln Elementary, they tore it down back in the eighties to build a strip mall, which is a shame, really, it had these beautiful big windows—but anyway, my teachers there, they always had the same thing to say to my parents. \n\nI remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She was this strict older lady, always wore these heavy wool cardigans even in May. She told my mother, she said, \"He's a bright boy, he really is, but he's just... scattered.\" That was her word, scattered. And she wasn't wrong. I was always, always losing my pencils. My dad must have bought me a hundred of those yellow Ticonderoga pencils a year. I’d set one down to look at a bug on the windowsill, and poof, it was gone. And I couldn't just sit quietly and raise my hand. If I knew the answer, or even if I just thought I knew it, I'd just blurt it right out. \"George Washington!\" \"The capital of Ohio is Columbus!\" I just couldn't hold it in. \n\nAnd finishing my work? Oh, boy. It wasn't that the work was too hard, it was just... there were so many other things to look at. The kid next to me tying his shoe, the clock ticking on the wall, a dog barking outside. Mrs. Gable would have to come stand right next to my desk, tap her finger on my paper, and say, \"You need to finish this math sheet before you can go out for recess.\" And of course, I wanted to go to recess more than anything in the world, because that meant running around and playing kickball, but I still needed that constant reminding just to get through ten multiplication problems. So, yeah, I guess that engine has been running hot for a very, very long time. It’s just who I am, really. Sure, the box ended up a little lopsided, and one corner is definitely sitting higher than the rest, but I figure the tomatoes are not going to care if their bed is perfectly square. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any sort of rigid system or need for things to be exact.
